\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{nx}\big(\uptau,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau)\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathsf{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)}&\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{1+\frac{\upgamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\otimes\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{1+\frac{\upgamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\otimes\boldsymbol{I}_{p}&\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{1+\upgamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\otimes\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)\times\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}

(7.17)

Since it holds that,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{1+\frac{\upgamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\to}\boldsymbol{0}\ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \frac{1}{n^{1+\upgamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\to}\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\ \ \ \text{when}\ \upgamma_{x}\in(0,1),

where the second limit follows by Lemma B3 in the Appendix of
Kostakis et al., 2015
. Therefore, for the case of mildly integrated regressors in the model we obtain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}^{qr}(\uptau)-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{qr}(\uptau)\big)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\displaystyle\sqrt{n}&0\\
0&\displaystyle n^{\frac{1+\upgamma_{x}}{2}}\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\end{bmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{qr}(\uptau)-\alpha(\uptau)\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{n}^{qr}(\uptau)-\boldsymbol{\beta}(\uptau)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\equiv\left\{\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\right\}^{-1}\times\left\{\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau)\big)\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)\times\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right\}^{-1}\times\mathcal{N}\left(0,\uptau(1-\uptau)\times\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}\times\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\times\mathcal{N}\left(0,\uptau(1-\uptau)\times\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\uptau(1-\uptau)}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}\times\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}^{-1}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right)

(7.18)

by an application of the continuous mapping theorem to the first term.
∎

Remark 8
.

Notice also that the limit results given by Corollary
3
can be used to prove the limiting distribution provided by Corollary
1
of the paper. This can be done, by employing the trick presented in the proof of Theorem 1 in
Xiao, 2009
. In particular, by linearizing the optimization function in terms of an arbitrary centered quantity
\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}(\uptau)-\boldsymbol{\theta}(\uptau)\right)
. Thus, using the convexity lemma we take the distributional limit of the linearized part and then minimize to get the desired expression as in (
7.10
).

Proof of Corollary
4

Proof.

For the IVX based estimation of the quantile regression model, we use the dequantile procedure proposed by
Lee, 2016
. Thus,
y_{t}(\uptau)=y_{t}-\alpha(\uptau)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2})
.
Furthermore, we employ the following embedded normalization matrices

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}:=\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}_{t-1,n}:=\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1},\ \ \ \text{where}\ \ \tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}:=n^{\frac{1+(\upgamma_{x}\wedge\upgamma_{z})}{2}}\boldsymbol{I}_{p}

(7.19)

Part
(i)

The limit holds regardless of the stochastic dominance of the exponent rates
\upgamma_{x}
and
\upgamma_{z}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}_{nz}\big(\uptau,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{ivx}_{n}(\uptau)\big)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau)\big)\equiv n^{\frac{1+(\gamma_{x}\wedge\gamma_{z})}{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau)\big)\otimes\boldsymbol{I}_{p}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau(1-\uptau)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big).

(7.20)

Part
(ii)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{M}_{nz}\big(\uptau,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{ivx}_{n}(\uptau)\big):=\sum_{t=1}^{n}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\Rightarrow f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}

(7.21)

which can be easily shown, since
\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\to}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}
.

Part
(iii)

Then,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\equiv\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, the limit result follows as below

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{M}_{nz}\big(\uptau,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{ivx}_{n}(\uptau)\big)

\displaystyle:=\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right]\equiv{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)\times\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}

Since, a convergence in probability holds
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\to}\mathbb{E}\big[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)}\big]=:f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)
. Moreover, we have that

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{n}^{ivx-qr}(\uptau)-\boldsymbol{\beta}(\uptau)\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\left\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{\prime}}\right\}^{-1}\times\left\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau)\big)\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\bigg\{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)\times\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\bigg\}^{-1}\times\mathcal{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau(1-\uptau)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}\times\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1}\times\mathcal{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau(1-\uptau)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\frac{\uptau(1-\uptau)}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1}\right)^{\prime}\right)\equiv\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\frac{\uptau(1-\uptau)}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\right).

The above result proves the Gaussian random variable limit given by Corollary
2
which holds for both the cases of local unit root and mildly integrated regressors in the quantile predictive regression model. Furthermore, in the case we employ the alternative IVX-QR estimator proposed by
Lee, 2016
(IVZ estimator); in which case the set of nonstationary regressors,
\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}
, is replaced by the mildly integrated instruments,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}
, we obtain the following limit result

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{n}^{ivz-qr}(\uptau)-\boldsymbol{\beta}(\uptau)\big)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\bigg\{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\bigg\}^{-1}\times\mathcal{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau(1-\uptau)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\times\mathcal{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau(1-\uptau)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\frac{\uptau(1-\uptau)}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\right)

(7.22)

since,
\sum_{t=1}^{n}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\Rightarrow f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}
, which is nuisance-parameter free for both the case of local unit root or mildly integrated regressors in the model.
∎

Remark 9
.

Notice that in a standard time series quantile regression with stationary covariates it holds that the regression
\uptau-
quantile is asymptotically normal, with

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\beta}(\uptau)\right)\overset{d}{\to}\mathcal{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau(1-\uptau)\boldsymbol{D}_{1}^{-1}(\uptau)\boldsymbol{D}_{0}\boldsymbol{D}_{1}^{-1}(\uptau)\big).

(7.23)

with an appropriate defined covariance matrix which is a function of the moments of the underline error distribution (see,
Goh and Knight, 2009
). Therefore, we can clearly see that under nonstationarity the covariance matrix of the Gaussian random variate is stochastic due to the presence of the nuisance parameter of persistence.

Remark 10
.

Notice that the objective function for the setting of the nonstationary quantile predictive regression model, becomes globally convex in the parameter. Hence, the method based on the convexity lemma by Pollard (1991) is applicable. Therefore, our proofs for the asymptotic theory of test of parameter restrictions is based on the asymptotic theory framework proposed by
Xiao, 2009
. Consequently, the limit results for the linear parameter restrictions can be employed when constructing the parameter specific restrictions that correspond to structural break tests.

Alternative IVZ-QR estimator

Following the framework proposed by
Lee, 2016
we also consider the limiting distribution of the IVX-QR estimator when the original persistent regressors are replaced by the instrumental variables in the optimization function. The particular approach is convenient as it significantly reduces the computational time by avoiding the nonconvex optimization procedure given by expression (
2.19
) which requires to use a grid search with several local optima. More specifically, we consider

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n}^{ivx-qr}\left(\uptau\right):=\underset{\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}{\mathsf{arg\ min}}\ \sum_{t=1}^{n}\uprho_{\tau}\big(y_{t}(\uptau)-\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\big).

(7.24)

Corollary 6
.

Under the null hypothesis
\mathcal{H}_{0}:\boldsymbol{\beta}(\uptau)=0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n}^{ivx-qr}(\uptau)-\boldsymbol{\beta}(\uptau)\big)\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\frac{\displaystyle\uptau(1-\uptau)}{\displaystyle f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\right)

(7.25)

both for near unit root and mildly integrated predictors, where
\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}=n^{\frac{1+\upgamma_{x}\wedge\upgamma_{z}}{2}}\boldsymbol{I}_{p}
.

Lemma 3
.

(Self-normalized IVX-QR) Under Assumption
1
it holds that,

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}}(0)^{2}}{\uptau(1-\uptau)}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{ivx-qr}_{n}(\uptau)-\boldsymbol{\beta}(\uptau)\right)^{\prime}\big(\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}\big)^{-1}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{ivx-qr}_{n}(\uptau)-\boldsymbol{\beta}(\uptau)\right)\Rightarrow\chi^{2}_{p}

(7.26)

such that
\widehat{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}}(0)^{2}
is a consistent estimator of
f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}
and
p
degrees of freedom.

Therefore, Lemma
3
provides a uniform inference limit result which allows to easily obtain critical values since is nuisance-parameter free. Furthermore, if we are interested to test for example the predictability of a specific subgroup among the predictors, say
\mathcal{H}_{0}:\beta_{1}(\uptau)=\beta_{2}(\uptau)=0
, then the formulation of the Wald statistic with the linear restrictions matrix can be employed. In the particular example, the restrictions matrix takes the form
\boldsymbol{R}=\big[\boldsymbol{I}_{2},\boldsymbol{0}_{2\times(p-2)}\big]
. Then, generalizing the specific example for testing a set of linear restrictions, implies that the null hypothesis is formulated as
\mathcal{H}_{0}:\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{\beta}(\uptau)=0
where
\boldsymbol{R}
is a
r\times p
known restriction matrix.

Then, the limiting distribution for the IVX-Wald statistic for the quantile predictive regression is given by the following expression

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}}(0)^{2}}{\uptau(1-\uptau)}\left(\boldsymbol{R}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{ivx-qr}_{n}(\uptau)\right)^{\prime}\bigg[\boldsymbol{R}\big(\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}\big)^{-1}\boldsymbol{R}^{\prime}\bigg]^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{R}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{ivx-qr}_{n}(\uptau)\right)\Rightarrow\chi^{2}_{p-2}

where
\chi^{2}_{p-2}
denotes the chi-square random variate with
(p-2)
degrees of freedom such that
\mathbb{P}\left(\chi^{2}\geq\chi^{2}_{p-2;\upalpha}\right)=\upalpha
, where
0<\upalpha<1
denotes the significance level.

Proof of Lemma
1

We have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{ivx-qr}_{n}(\uptau)=\frac{\widehat{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}}(0)^{2}}{\uptau(1-\uptau)}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivx-qr}_{n}(\uptau)-\boldsymbol{\beta}(\uptau)\right)^{\prime}\bigg(\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{P}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\boldsymbol{X}\bigg)\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivx-qr}_{n}(\uptau)-\boldsymbol{\beta}(\uptau)\right)\Rightarrow\chi^{2}_{p}

(7.27)

where

\displaystyle\bigg(\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{P}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\boldsymbol{X}\bigg):=\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)^{-1}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}\right)\equiv\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}^{\prime}_{t-1}\right)\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}^{\prime}_{t-1}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}^{\prime}_{t-1}\right)^{\prime}

Moreover, we use the embedded normalization matrices such that

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\right)\times\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}_{t-1,n}\right)\equiv\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}_{t-1,n}\right)\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\to}f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}

and the fact that
\displaystyle\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{z}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}^{\prime}_{t-1}\right)\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\to}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}
.

Proof.

From Corollary
2
we have that

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{n}^{ivx-qr}\left(\uptau\right)-\boldsymbol{\beta}\left(\uptau\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}_{t-1,n}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau)\big)\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\left(0,\frac{\uptau(1-\uptau)}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}\times\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)^{-1}\right)

Denote the consistent sample estimator of
\widehat{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}}(0)
, with
f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)
where

\displaystyle\widehat{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}}(0)=\sum_{t=1}^{n}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)

(7.28)

Therefore, the following asymptotic convergence result follows

\displaystyle\bigg(\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{P}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\boldsymbol{X}\bigg)\Rightarrow\big[f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\big]\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\times\big[f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\big]^{\prime}\equiv f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{ivx-qr}_{n}(\uptau)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{1}{\uptau(1-\uptau)}\times\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\left(0,\frac{\uptau(1-\uptau)}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}\times\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}^{-1}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \times\bigg\{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}\times\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \times\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\left(0,\frac{\uptau(1-\uptau)}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}\times\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}^{-1}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{ivx-qr}_{n}(\uptau)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}{\uptau(1-\uptau)}\frac{\uptau(1-\uptau)}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}\times\bigg[\mathcal{N}\left(0,\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}^{-1}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)^{-1}\right)\bigg]^{\prime}

\displaystyle\ \ \times\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)^{-1}\times\bigg[\mathcal{N}\left(0,\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}^{-1}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)^{-1}\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(0,1)\left[\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}^{-1}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)^{-1/2}\right]^{\prime}\times\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)\times\left[\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}^{-1}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)^{-1/2}\right]\mathcal{N}(0,1)

\displaystyle=\big[\mathcal{N}(0,1)\big]^{2}\equiv\chi^{2}_{2}.

which is a standard
\chi^{2}-
distribution with
2
degrees of freedom.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1

Proof.

Part (
i
)

First we consider the limit expression for the case of mildly integrated regressors

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle:=\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{n}\left(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\right)-\lambda\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{n}\left(1,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\right)^{-1/2}\left\{\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)-\lambda\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right\}^{-1/2}\left\{\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\lambda\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right)-\lambda\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\left\{\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right\}^{-1/2}\left\{\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{0},\lambda\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right)-\lambda\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\left\{\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right\}^{-1/2}\left\{\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right\}^{1/2}\times\bigg\{\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{0},\lambda\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\right)-\lambda\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\right)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\equiv\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\bigg[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\bigg].

Therefore, for some
0<\lambda<1
and
\uptau_{0}\in(0,1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{SQ}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\Rightarrow\underset{\lambda\in[0,1]}{\mathsf{sup}}\ \big\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p}(\lambda)\big\|_{\infty}.

(7.29)

which is a nuisance-parameter free distribution that holds under the null hypothesis. In summary, suppose that the data are generated by the quantile predictive regression model and Assumptions
1
-
2
are satisfied. Then, under the null null hypothesis
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{(A)}
the fluctuation type statistics weakly converge to the limiting distribution given by expression (
7.29
) for mildly integrated regressors for some unknown break-point location
0<\lambda<1
.

Second, for local unit root regressors (high persistence) then, following limit holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle:=\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{n}\left(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\right)-\lambda\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{n}\left(1,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\right)^{-1/2}\left\{\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)-\lambda\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\begin{bmatrix}1&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)^{\prime}\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}^{-1/2}\times\left\{\begin{bmatrix}B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}\end{bmatrix}-\lambda\begin{bmatrix}B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(1)\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}\end{bmatrix}\right\}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)^{\prime}\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}^{-1/2}\times\begin{bmatrix}B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)-\lambda B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(1)\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}-\lambda\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(1\times p)}}\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(p\times 1)}}&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(p\times p)}}\end{bmatrix}^{-1/2}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(p+1)\times(p+1)}}\times\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{\mathcal{BB}}_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(1\times n)}}\\
\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{JB}}_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(p\times n)}}\end{bmatrix}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(p+1)\times n}}

(7.30)

where
\mathbf{\mathcal{BB}}_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda):=B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)-\lambda B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(1)
and
\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{JB}}_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda):=\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}-\lambda\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}
. Thus, under the null hypothesis the OLS based functional with local unit root regressors converges into a nonstandard and nonpivotal limiting distribution.

Part (
ii
)

The limit expression of the fluctuation type test based on the IVX estimator is given as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle:=\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{n}\left(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivx}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\right)-\lambda\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{n}\left(1,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivx}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}_{t-1,n}\right)^{-1/2}\left\{\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)-\lambda\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}_{t-1}\right]\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\right)^{-1/2}\left\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)-\lambda\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1/2}\times\bigg\{\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\bigg(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\bigg)-\lambda\mathcal{N}\bigg(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\bigg)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1/2}\times\bigg\{\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\bigg(\boldsymbol{0},\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\bigg)-\lambda\mathcal{N}\bigg(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\bigg)\bigg\}\equiv\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\big].

provided that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\equiv\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}
, which applies when
\upgamma_{x}\in(0,\upgamma_{z})
. Proposition
1

(
ii
)
shows that the limiting distribution of the fluctuation type test is nonstandard in general when the IVX estimator is employed since we employ limit results which hold for both LUR and MI regressors. However, when the coefficient of persistence of regressors has an exponent rate with an absolute value less than the exponent rate of the mildly integrated instruments, then the asymptotic covariance matrix of the Gaussian variant has a simpified form due to the stochastic dominance property of these covariance matrices.

Part (
iii
)

We obtain the following limit result which holds for both LUR and MI regressors

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle:=\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{ivz}_{n}\left(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivz}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\right)-\lambda\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{ivz}_{n}\left(1,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivz}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=\big(\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}\big)^{-1/2}\left\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\left(y_{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivz}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\right)-\lambda\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\left(y_{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivz}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\right)^{-1/2}\left\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(y_{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\big)-\lambda\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(y_{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1/2}\times\bigg\{\mathcal{N}\bigg(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\bigg)-\lambda\mathcal{N}\bigg(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\bigg)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1/2}\times\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\bigg\{\mathcal{N}\bigg(\boldsymbol{0},\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\bigg)-\lambda\mathcal{N}\bigg(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\bigg)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\ \boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1/2}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}\times\bigg\{\mathcal{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\lambda\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\big)-\lambda\mathcal{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\big)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\bigg[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\bigg],\ \ \text{since}\ \boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1/2}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}=\boldsymbol{I}_{p}.

which implies that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}}\bigg[\mathcal{J}_{n}\big(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivz}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\big)-\lambda\mathcal{J}_{n}\big(1,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivz}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\big)\bigg]\Rightarrow\frac{\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}}{\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}}\bigg[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\bigg]

Thus, for some
0<\lambda<1
and
\uptau_{0}\in(0,1)
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{SQ}^{ivz}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\Rightarrow\underset{\lambda\in[0,1]}{\mathsf{sup}}\ \big\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p}(\lambda)\big\|_{\infty}

(7.31)

Overall, it seems that the fluctuation type statistics are non-pivotal, at the first glance, for all estimators and across the two persistence classes, due to the dependence of their limiting distributions on the nuisance coefficient of persistence; appearing in the estimation of the covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}
of the Gaussian variant that the corresponding partial sum processes converge to. However, the IVZ based statistic for both types of persistence induce an approximation which weakly converges into a Brownian bridge type limit. In practise, when we utilize the IVZ estimator (see also Theorem 3.2 and Proposition 3.2 in
Lee, 2016
) then the limiting distribution of the moment matrix between the covariates and the residuals of the model simplifies, and thus the overall limit is nuisance-parameter free. Furthermore, a similar limit for the IVX based test hold in the case of mildly integrated regressors. A nonstandard limit distribution appears in the case of high persistence (e.g., LUR) for both the OLS and IVX based test statistics.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2

We need to prove the following limit result

\displaystyle\mathcal{SW}^{ols}_{n}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\underset{\lambda\in[0,1]}{\mathsf{sup}}\ \frac{\ \big\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p+1}(\lambda)\ \big\|^{2}}{\lambda(1-\lambda)}

(7.32)

where the exponent rate that captures the degree of persistence for the original regressors can be
\upgamma_{x}=1
or
\upgamma_{x}\in(0,1)
. In particular to prove the Brownian Bridge limit, we need to derive the limiting distribution of the term
\mathcal{J}_{nx}\big(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\big)-\lambda\mathcal{J}_{nx}\big(1,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\big)
.

Using Definition
3
, for the case of mildly integrated regressors,
\upgamma_{x}\in(0,1)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{ols}_{nx}\big(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\big)

\displaystyle:=\big(\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}\big)^{-1/2}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\left(y_{t}-\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\bigg[\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(1)\bigg].

(7.33)

where
\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(.)
is a
p-
vector of independent Wiener processes and the convergence holds because
\big\{\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(y_{t}-\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\big)\big\}
is a sequence of martingale differences under the null. The particular limit is derived in the proof of Proposition
1
.

Part
(i)

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}=\big(\boldsymbol{1},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}_{t-1}\big)^{\prime}
the design matrix and
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\uptau_{0})=\big(\alpha(\uptau_{0}),\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}(\uptau_{0})\big)^{\prime}
to be the parameter vector. Then, to derive the asymptotic distribution of the OLS-Wald test we employ the following functional

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{nx}^{ols}\big(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\big):=\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big),\ \ \text{for some}\ \ 0<\lambda<1,

(7.34)

where
u_{t}(\uptau_{0})=\big(y_{t}-\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\big)
with
\uptau_{0}\in(0,1)
. Then, the OLS based estimator for the subsample
1\leq t\leq\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor
denoted with
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})
is given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}y_{t}\right)

(7.35)

Therefore, it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\hat{S}_{nx}^{ols}\left(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\right)\bigg\|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\ \boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\bigg[\mathds{1}\big\{y_{t}=\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\big\}\bigg]\ \bigg\|

\displaystyle\leq(p+1)D_{n}^{-1}\underset{1\leq i\leq n}{\mathsf{max}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\right\rVert\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\to}0.

(7.36)

which implies that
\hat{S}_{nx}^{ols}\left(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\right)=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
.

Moreover, for the estimator of the first subsample we obtain the following expression

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{ols}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{p}{\to}\bigg\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}\lambda\mathbb{V}_{xx}\bigg\}^{-1}\times S_{nx}^{ols}\big(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\big)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle\equiv{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}}\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1}\times\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\lambda\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}}\frac{1}{\lambda}\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1}\times\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}^{1/2}\times\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{0},\lambda\big)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}}\frac{1}{\lambda}\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1/2}\times\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda).

(7.37)

since it holds that
S_{x}^{ols}\big(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}(\uptau_{0})\big)\equiv\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\lambda\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right)
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{2}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{p}{\to}\bigg\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}(1-\lambda)\mathbb{V}_{xx}\bigg\}^{-1}\times S_{nx}^{ols}\big(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\big)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle\equiv{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}}\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1/2}\times\bigg[\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(1)-\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda)\bigg].

(7.38)

Therefore, combining (
7.1.7
) and (
7.1.7
) we obtain the following expression

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\left[\Delta\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}\left(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\right)\right]\Rightarrow-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}}\frac{1}{\lambda(1-\lambda)}\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1/2}\times\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(1)\big].

Moreover, the convergence of the covariance matrix follows as below

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\equiv\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\times\bigg\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}^{ols}_{1n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})+\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}^{ols}_{2n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg\}

(7.39)

Therefore, it holds that

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}^{ols}_{1n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})

(7.40)

\displaystyle=\left\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{L}}_{nx}\left(\uptau_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{ols}(\uptau_{0})\right)}\right\}^{-1}\left\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\right\}\left\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{L}}_{nx}\left(\uptau_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{ols}(\uptau_{0})\right)}\right\}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}}\right\}^{-1}\left\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\times\left\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}}\right\}^{-1}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigg\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}\lambda\mathbb{V}_{xx}\bigg\}^{-1}\times\bigg\{\lambda\mathbb{V}_{xx}\bigg\}\times\bigg\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}\mathbb{V}_{xx}\bigg\}^{-1}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}}\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1}

(7.41)

Similarly, we obtain that

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}^{ols}_{2n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}}\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1}

(7.42)

Thus,

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\equiv{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}}\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\right\}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}}\frac{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}{\lambda(1-\lambda)}\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1}

(7.43)

which implies that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{n}^{ols}\left(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\right)

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\bigg\{\Delta\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg\}^{\prime}\bigg[\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg]^{-1}\bigg\{\Delta\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}}\frac{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}{\left[\lambda(1-\lambda)\right]^{2}}\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(1)\big]^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1/2}\right)^{\prime}\times\left\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}\frac{\lambda(1-\lambda)}{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\mathbb{V}_{xx}\right\}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \times\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1/2}\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(1)\big]

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{\lambda(1-\lambda)}\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(1)\big]^{\prime}\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(1)\big].

(7.44)

Hence, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{SW}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})

\displaystyle:=\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\eta}}{\mathsf{sup}}\ \boldsymbol{D}_{n}\left\{\Delta\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\right\}^{\prime}\times\bigg[\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg]^{-1}\times\left\{\Delta\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\eta}}{\mathsf{sup}}\ \frac{\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(1)\big]^{\prime}\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(1)\big]}{\lambda(1-\lambda)}

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\eta}}{\mathsf{sup}}\ \frac{\big\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p+1}(\lambda)\big\|^{2}}{\lambda(1-\lambda)},\ \text{for}\ \upgamma_{x}\in(0,1),

(7.45)

where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p+1}(\lambda)
is a Brownian Bridge process, which holds for the case of mildly integrated regressors, that is,
\upgamma_{x}\in(0,1)
and holds even under the presence of model intercept. Next, we provide of some auxiliary derivations employed for Part
(i)
.
∎

Proof.

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}y_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\big[\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})+\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\big]\right)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})+\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Thus,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\right)=\left(\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

which implies that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\big\{\lambda\mathbb{V}_{xx}\big\}^{-1}\times\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\lambda\mathbb{V}_{xx}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{\lambda}\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1}\times\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{1/2}\times\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1/2}\times\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda).

(7.46)

Recall that

\displaystyle S_{nx}^{ols}\big(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\big):=\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big),\ \ \text{for}\ (\lambda,\uptau_{0})\in(0,1),

where
u_{t}(\uptau_{0})=\big(y_{t}-\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\big)
. Thus,
S_{nx}^{ols}\big(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\big)\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\lambda\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}\right)
.
Denote with
\mathbb{V}_{xx}:=\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\end{bmatrix}
, which implies that

\displaystyle S_{nx}^{ols}\big(\lambda,\uptau_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{ols}_{n}(\uptau_{0})\big)\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\lambda\mathbb{V}_{xx}\big)\equiv\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1/2}\times\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda).

Similarly, we can prove that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{2}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\mathbb{V}_{xx}^{-1/2}\times\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(1)-\boldsymbol{W}_{p+1}(\lambda)\big].

Practically, the above results can be deduced from Assumption
2

(b)
such that

\displaystyle\underset{r\in[0,\lambda]}{\mathsf{sup}}\left|\frac{1}{n^{1+\upgamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}-r\mathbb{E}\big[\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\big]\right|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),\ \text{as}\ n\to\infty.

(7.47)

where
\mathbb{E}\big[\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\big]\equiv\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}
, which implies that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{1+\upgamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\overset{p}{\to}\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\underset{r\in(\lambda,1]}{\mathsf{sup}}\left|\frac{1}{n^{1+\upgamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor+1}^{n}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}-r\mathbb{E}\big[\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\big]\right|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),\ \text{as}\ n\to\infty.

(7.48)

which implies that
\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{1+\upgamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor+1}^{n}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\overset{p}{\to}(1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}
.

∎

Part
(ii)

Next, we investigate the asymptotic behaviour of the OLS-Wald test statistic in the case of local unit root regressors (i.e., high persistent). To minimize complexity of notation for the derivations of this proof we denote with

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{xx}:=\begin{bmatrix}1&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)^{\prime}\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda):=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)^{\prime}\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}

(7.49)

Moreover, it holds that
\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\right)\Rightarrow\mathbb{S}_{xx}
and

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\right)\Rightarrow\begin{bmatrix}\lambda&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)^{\prime}\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)^{\prime}\end{bmatrix},\text{when}\ \upgamma_{x}=1.

(7.50)

Proof.

Therefore, for LUR regressors,
\upgamma_{x}=1
, we have that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\right)=\left(\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

which implies that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{xx}^{-1}(\lambda)\times\begin{bmatrix}B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}\end{bmatrix}

(7.51)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{2}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\big(\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{xx}(1)-\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\big)^{-1}\times\begin{bmatrix}B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(1)-B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}\end{bmatrix}

(7.52)

Therefore, combining (
7.51
) and (
7.52
) we obtain the following expression

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\left[\Delta\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}\left(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\begin{bmatrix}\displaystyle\big[\mathbb{S}_{xx}(1)-\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\big]^{-1}\times\bigg\{B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(1)-B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)\bigg\}-\mathbb{S}^{-1}_{xx}(\lambda)\times B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)\\
\displaystyle\big[\mathbb{S}_{xx}(1)-\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\big]^{-1}\times\bigg\{\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}-\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}\bigg\}-\mathbb{S}^{-1}_{xx}(\lambda)\times\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\equiv\big[\mathbb{S}_{xx}(1)-\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\big]^{-1}\times\begin{bmatrix}\displaystyle B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(1)-B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}-\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}\end{bmatrix}-\mathbb{S}^{-1}_{xx}(\lambda)\times\begin{bmatrix}\displaystyle B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}\end{bmatrix}

(7.53)

Then, the OLS-Wald test for testing the null hypothesis of no parameter instability in the nonstationary quantile predictive regression model at an unknown break-point location
\kappa=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor
is given by the following expression

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{n}^{ols}\left(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\right):=\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\bigg\{\Delta\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg\}^{\prime}\bigg[\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg]^{-1}\bigg\{\Delta\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg\}

(7.54)

Furthermore, we study the limiting variance of the OLS-Wald test under the null hypothesis. In particular, the convergence of the covariance matrix follows as below

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\

\displaystyle\bigg\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}^{ols}_{1n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg\}

\displaystyle\equiv\left\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{L}_{nx}\left(\uptau_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{ols}(\uptau_{0})\right)}\right\}^{-1}\left\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\ \boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\right\}\left\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{L}_{nx}\left(\uptau_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{ols}(\uptau_{0})\right)}\right\}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}}\right\}^{-1}\times\left\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\ \boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\times\left\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}f_{u_{t}(\uptau),t-1}(0)\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\boldsymbol{D}_{n}^{-1}}\right\}^{-1}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigg\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\bigg\}^{-1}\times\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\times\bigg\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)}\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\bigg\}^{-1}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}}\mathbb{S}_{xx}^{-1}(\lambda).

(7.55)

and

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\ \bigg\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}^{ols}_{2n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg\}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}}\bigg[\mathbb{S}_{xx}(1)-\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\bigg]^{-1}.

(7.56)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\ \bigg\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg\}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}}\bigg\{\mathbb{S}_{xx}^{-1}(\lambda)+\bigg[\mathbb{S}_{xx}(1)-\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\bigg]^{-1}\bigg\}.

(7.57)

We can also simplify further the term
\bigg\{\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{plim}}\ \widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg\}^{-1}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}\bigg[\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)-\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\mathbb{S}^{-1}_{xx}(1)\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\bigg]
.
Denote with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{ols}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big):=\big[\mathbb{S}_{xx}(1)-\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\big]^{-1}\times\begin{bmatrix}\displaystyle B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(1)-B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}-\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}\end{bmatrix}-\mathbb{S}^{-1}_{xx}(\lambda)\times\begin{bmatrix}\displaystyle B_{\psi_{\uptau}}(\lambda)\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)dB_{\psi_{\uptau}}\end{bmatrix}

Then, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{n}^{ols}\left(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\right)

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{D}_{n}\bigg\{\Delta\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg\}^{\prime}\bigg[\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg]^{-1}\bigg\{\Delta\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{ols}_{n}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{ols}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)^{\prime}\big[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\big]\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{ols}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f_{u_{t}(\uptau)}(0)^{2}}\bigg\{\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{ols}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\bigg\}^{\prime}\bigg[\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)-\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\mathbb{S}^{-1}_{xx}(1)\mathbb{S}_{xx}(\lambda)\bigg]\bigg\{\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{ols}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\bigg\}.

∎

Therefore, the asymptotic distribution of the sup OLS-Wald test statistic is nonpivotal and nonstandard and has the above analytical expression. Furthermore, we verify that a trivial aspect such as the inclusion of a model intercept can complicate the asymptotic theory of the structural break test since the model intercept and the slopes are known to have different rates of convergence.

Proof of Theorem
1

\displaystyle\mathcal{SW}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\Rightarrow\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\eta}}{\mathsf{sup}}\bigg\{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)^{\prime}\big[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\big]^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\bigg\}

(7.58)

where
\Lambda_{\eta}:=[\eta,1-\eta]
with
0<\eta<1/2
and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)

(7.59)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle:=\lambda\big(\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}(\lambda)\big)\big(\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}(\lambda)\big)^{\prime}+(1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}(\lambda)^{\prime}

(7.60)

such that

\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}(\lambda)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\left(\lambda\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{xx}+\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}^{\mu}(r)d\boldsymbol{J}_{c}^{\prime}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{xx}+\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}^{\mu}_{c}(r)d\boldsymbol{J}_{c}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}&,\text{for}\ \upgamma_{x}=1\\
\\
\displaystyle\lambda\boldsymbol{I}_{p}&,\text{for}\ \upgamma_{x}\in(0,1)\end{cases}

where
\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(.)
is a
p-
dimensional standard Brownian motion,
\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{\lambda}e^{(\lambda-s)\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}d\boldsymbol{B}(s)
is an
Ornstein-Uhkenbeck
process and we denote with
\boldsymbol{J}^{\mu}_{c}(\lambda)=\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(\lambda)-\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(s)ds
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{p}^{\mu}(\lambda)=\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{W}(s)ds
the demeaned processes of
\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(\lambda)
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)
respectively.

Proof.

We consider the limiting distribution of the sup IVX-Wald test for the nonstationary quantile predictive regression model. An analytic expression for the limiting variance of the IVX-Wald test statistic,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big):=\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)+\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)
, can be obtained by formulating the test statistic with respect to the linear restrictions matrix, under the null hypothesis of no structural break for a fixed quantile
\uptau_{0}\in(0,1)
. Furthermore, by applying weakly convergence arguments to the particular expression (with zero off-diagonal terms) holds due to the orthogonality property of the regressors of the two regimes with respect to the unknown break-location
\kappa=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor
,
0<\lambda<1
.

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle:=\bigg[\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\bigg]\begin{bmatrix}\left(\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{1}\right)^{-1}&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\left(\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{2}\right)^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}\right)^{-1}&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{2}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}\right)^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\\
-\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigg[\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)+\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\bigg]

(7.61)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle:=\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{1}\right)^{-1}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{1}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{1}\right)^{-1}

(7.62)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle:=\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{2}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{2}\right)^{-1}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{2}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{2}\right)^{-1}

(7.63)

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left[\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)+\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\right]\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

(7.64)

Therefore, by employing the corresponding normalized versions it holds that

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle:=\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1,n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\right)\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\equiv\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right)\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

(7.65)

Moreover, we denote the weakly convergence of the following moment matrices as below

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(1)

(7.66)

By applying weak convergence arguments we obtain that

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\times\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\times\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)^{-1}\big(\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)^{\prime}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle\equiv\lambda\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)^{\prime}\right)^{-1}

(7.67)

Similarly, it holds that

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle:=\left(\sum_{t=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor+1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1,n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{t=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor+1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)\left(\sum_{t=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor+1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor+1}^{n}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor+1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\right)\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor+1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\equiv\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left(\sum_{t=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor+1}^{n}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{t=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor+1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right)\left(\sum_{t=\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor+1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

Thus, it follows that

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\times\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\times\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(1)-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\big)^{-1}\big((1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)\left[\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(1)-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\big)^{\prime}\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle\equiv(1-\lambda)\bigg\{\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(1)-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\big)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(1)-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\big)^{\prime}\bigg\}^{-1}

(7.68)

Therefore, for the limiting variance of the sup IVX-Wald test we have that

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left[\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)+\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\right]\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\lambda\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}(\lambda)^{-1}+(1-\lambda)\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(1)-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\big)^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{cxz}(1)-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}(\lambda)\big)^{-1}\bigg\}

Furthermore, it holds that

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivx}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(\lambda)\times\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(\lambda)\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}\times\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda).

(7.69)

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}^{ivx}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau)\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\bigg(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(1)-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\bigg)^{-1}\times\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})(1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\bigg(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(1)-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\bigg)^{-1}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}\times\bigg[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)-\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)\bigg].

(7.70)

which implies that

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}^{ivx}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}^{ivx}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\right)\Rightarrow-\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}\bigg\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathcal{A}}\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathcal{B}}\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\bigg\}

(7.71)

where

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathcal{A}}=\left\{\bigg(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(1)-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\bigg)^{-1}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(\lambda)\right\}\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathcal{B}}=\bigg(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(1)-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\bigg)^{-1}

(7.72)

Therefore, putting all the above together the expression can be simplified as below

\displaystyle\mathcal{SW}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\Rightarrow\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(1)\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\bigg)^{\prime}

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{\lambda\big(\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(1)\big)\big(\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(1)\big)^{\prime}+(1-\lambda)\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(1)\right)\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(1)\right)^{\prime}\bigg\}^{-1}

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(1)\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\bigg).

Denote with

\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}(\lambda):=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(1)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\left(\lambda\mathbf{\Omega}_{xx}+\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}^{\mu}_{c}(r)d\boldsymbol{J}_{c}^{\prime}\right)\left(\mathbf{\Omega}_{xx}+\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}^{\mu}_{c}(r)d\boldsymbol{J}_{c}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}&,\text{for}\ \gamma_{x}=1\\
\\
\displaystyle\lambda\boldsymbol{I}_{p}&,\text{for}\ 0<\gamma_{x}<1.\end{cases}

Thus, in summary, the sup IVX-Wald statistic can be formulated as below

\displaystyle\mathcal{SW}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\eta}}{\mathsf{sup}}\bigg\{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)^{\prime}\big[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\big]^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle:=\lambda\big(\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}(\lambda)\big)\big(\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}(\lambda)\big)^{\prime}+(1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}(\lambda)^{\prime}

∎

Proof of Expression (
7.1.8
):

Proof.

We consider the dequantiled model with
p
regressors which can be either mildly integrated or high persistent. Then, by employing the embedded normalization matrices we can derive analytic expressions for the IVX estimators of the two subsamples.

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}^{ivx}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}y_{t}(\uptau)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\ +\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})+\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

Therefore, it holds that

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}^{ivx}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau)\right)=\left({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

Notice that it holds that

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}^{-1}_{n}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}^{-1}_{n}}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\right)^{\prime}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}^{-1}_{n}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}^{-1}_{n}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{1}{n^{1+(\upgamma_{x}\wedge\upgamma_{z})}}\otimes\boldsymbol{I}_{p}}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(\lambda)

(7.73)

For the second term of the expression for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}^{ivx}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau)\right)
it holds that

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right)

\displaystyle=\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right)

\displaystyle=\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n^{-\frac{1+(\upgamma_{x}\wedge\upgamma_{z})}{2}}\otimes\boldsymbol{I}_{p}}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}\bigg(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\bigg)

(7.74)

which implies that

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivx}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(\lambda)\times\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{-1}_{cxz}(\lambda)\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}\times\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda).

(7.75)

where
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)
corresponds to the weakly convergence argument of the partial sum process expression such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)\equiv\begin{cases}\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{C}_{z}^{-1}\left(\lambda\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{xx}+\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)d\boldsymbol{J}_{c}^{\prime}\right)&\ \text{if}\ \upgamma_{x}=1,\\
-\lambda\boldsymbol{C}_{z}^{-1}\bigg(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{xx}+\boldsymbol{C}_{p}\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\bigg)&\ \text{if}\ \upgamma_{x}\in(\upgamma_{z},1),\\
\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}&\ \text{if}\ \upgamma_{x}\in(0,\upgamma_{z}).\end{cases}

(7.76)

Following similar arguments expression (
7.1.8
) follows, and the rest of the arguments for the proof of Theorem
1
.
∎

Known break-point:

Proof.

For a known-break point the asymptotic term that corresponds to the case of high persistence,
\upgamma_{x}=1
, can simplify further, when
\lambda
is fixed say
\lambda\equiv\lambda_{0}
, such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda_{0})

\displaystyle\equiv\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{C}_{z}^{-1}\left(\lambda_{0}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{xx}+\int_{0}^{\lambda_{0}}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)d\boldsymbol{J}_{c}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle-\lambda_{0}\boldsymbol{C}_{z}^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{xx}+\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)d\boldsymbol{J}_{c}^{\prime}\right)\equiv\lambda_{0}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(1).

(7.77)

Thus, further generalizations occur based on the formulation of Theorem
1
, which cover both the case of mildly integrated and persistent regressors, demonstrating the convergence to a nuisance-free limiting distribution. In particular,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\big\{\lambda_{0}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\big\}^{-1}\times\big\{\lambda_{0}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big\}\times\big\{\lambda_{0}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big\}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\right)\equiv\bigg(\lambda_{0}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\bigg)^{-1}

(7.78)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\big\{(1-\lambda_{0})\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\big\}^{-1}\times\big\{(1-\lambda_{0})\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big\}\times\big\{(1-\lambda_{0})\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big\}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{0}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\right)\equiv\bigg((1-\lambda_{0})\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\bigg)^{-1}

(7.79)

Therefore, it follows that

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big)\Rightarrow\left\{\bigg(\lambda_{0}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\bigg)^{-1}+\bigg((1-\lambda_{0})\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\bigg)^{-1}\right\}

(7.80)

and by denoting with
\mathbb{S}_{cxz}:=\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)
we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big)\Rightarrow\bigg\{\big(\lambda_{0}\mathbb{S}_{cxz}\big)^{-1}+\big((1-\lambda_{0})\mathbb{S}_{cxz}\big)^{-1}\bigg\}\equiv\bigg\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}}+\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{0}}\bigg\}\times\mathbb{S}_{cxz}^{-1}

(7.81)

Then, the expression for the IVX-Wald test statistic given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big)\Rightarrow\bigg\{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{0}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big)^{\prime}\big[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big)\big]^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{0}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big)\bigg\}

(7.82)

can be simplified further by combining the asymptotic convergence results above

\displaystyle\Delta\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivx}_{n}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{0}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big):=\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}(1-\lambda_{0})}\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}\times\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda_{0})-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\big].

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big):=\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}(1-\lambda_{0})}\times\mathbb{S}_{cxz}^{-1}.

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big)\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\bigg\{\frac{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}{\lambda_{0}(1-\lambda_{0})}\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda_{0})-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\big]^{\prime}\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1}\right)^{\prime}\mathbb{S}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda_{0})-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\big]\bigg\}

(7.83)

Consider the formulation for the covariance matrix such that

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1}\right)^{\prime}\mathbb{S}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}\equiv\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1}\right)^{\prime}\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{\prime}\big)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}=\boldsymbol{I}_{p}.
