In summary, we prove that the asymptotic distribution of the IVX-Wald test for a known break-point
\lambda_{0}
is nuisance-parameter free as below

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\mathcal{W}_{n}^{ivx}\big(\lambda_{0};\uptau_{0}\big)\Rightarrow\frac{\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda_{0})-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\big]^{\prime}\big[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda_{0})-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\big]}{\lambda_{0}(1-\lambda_{0})}\Rightarrow\chi^{2}_{p}.

(7.84)

which holds regardless of the degree of persistence driving the regressors of the model (at least with respect to high persistence or mildly integrated regressors).

∎

Proof of Corollary
5

Proof.

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}^{ivz}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}y_{t}(\uptau)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\bigg[\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})+\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\bigg]\right)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})+\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

Therefore, it holds that

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}^{ivz}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau)\right)=\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{t-1,n}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\right)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

which implies that

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivz}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\big(\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)^{-1}\times\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{\lambda}\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{1/2}\times\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1/2}\times\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda).

(7.85)

Similarly, it can be proved that

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}^{ivz}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}(\uptau)\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\big((1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)^{-1}\times\mathcal{N}\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{0},\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})(1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1/2}\times\bigg[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\bigg].

(7.86)

Furthermore, it holds that

\displaystyle\tilde{\Delta}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivz}_{n}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)

\displaystyle:=\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\bigg[\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}^{ivz}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}^{ivz}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\bigg]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow-\frac{1}{\lambda(1-\lambda)}\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1/2}\times\bigg[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\bigg].

(7.87)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)^{-1}\big(\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\big)\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)^{\prime}\big)^{-1}\equiv\lambda\left(\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}

Therefore, the limiting variance for the IVZ estimator is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}^{ivz}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\bigg[\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)+\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\bigg]\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\right\}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1}

(7.88)

and

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}^{ivz}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\lambda(1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}

(7.89)

Then, the limiting distribution of the IVZ-Wald test is given by

\displaystyle\left\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivz}_{2}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivz}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\right)\right\}^{\prime}\left[\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{n}^{ivz}\big(\lambda;\uptau_{0}\big)\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\right]^{-1}\left\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivz}_{2}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{ivz}_{1}(\lambda;\uptau_{0})\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}{\big[\lambda(1-\lambda)\big]^{2}}\bigg[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\bigg]^{\prime}\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1/2}\times\lambda(1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}^{-1/2}\bigg[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\bigg]

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}{\lambda(1-\lambda)}\bigg[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\bigg]^{\prime}\bigg[\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(\lambda)-\lambda\boldsymbol{W}_{p}(1)\bigg]

(7.90)

Specifically, the IVZ-based test statistic shows an equivalent asymptotic behaviour with the corresponding IVX-based test when testing for structural breaks in nonstationary quantile predictive regressions.
∎

Remark 11
.

The formulation of the IVZ-Wald statistic shows the equivalence of an OLS-Wald based test to the IVZ-Wald statistic, since the IVZ estimation is equivalent to when original regressors are converted into stationary time series and then constructing an OLS based test; in which case we avoid any possible computational complexity of the second stage regression which requires the estimation of long-run covariance matrices to filter out potential long-run bias effects in the limiting variance of the test.

Asymptotic results on stochastic integrals

In this section we summarize main invariance principles employed for deriving some of the theoretical results of the paper. Extensive details on these results can be found in the framework proposed by
Phillips and Magdalinos, 2009
.

Lemma 4
.

Let
\mathbf{V}_{xx}:=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{r\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}\mathbf{\Omega}_{xx}e^{r\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}dr
where
\mathbf{\Omega}_{xx}
is the long-run covariance of the error term
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}
. Then, under the null hypothesis of no structural break in the predictive regression model the following large sample theory holds:

(i)

the sample covariance weakly convergence to the following limit (see Corollary
4
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\uptau_{0}(1-\uptau_{0})}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\psi_{\uptau}\big(u_{t}(\uptau_{0})\big)\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mathcal{U}}_{p}\left(\lambda\right)

(7.91)

where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{U}}_{p}\left(.\right)
is a Brownian motion with variance
\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}
as defined below

\boldsymbol{V}_{cxz}\equiv\begin{cases}\mathbf{V}_{zz}=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{r\boldsymbol{C}_{z}}\mathbf{\Omega}_{xx}e^{r\boldsymbol{C}_{z}}dr&,\ \text{when}\ 0<\upgamma_{z}<\upgamma_{x}<1,\\
\\
\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{xx}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{r\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}\mathbf{\Omega}_{xx}e^{r\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}dr&,\ \text{when}\ 0<\upgamma_{x}<\upgamma_{z}<1.\end{cases}

(7.92)

the sample covariance weakly convergence to the following limit

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\lambda n\rfloor}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\right]\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{n}^{-1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\equiv\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda)}

(7.93)

where the exact analytic form of
\boldsymbol{\varPsi}(\lambda)
depends on which of the two exponents rates of persistence stochastically dominates such as

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{cxz}(\lambda):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{C}_{z}^{-1}\left(\lambda\mathbf{\Omega}_{xx}+\int_{0}^{\lambda}\boldsymbol{J}^{\mu}_{c}(r)d\boldsymbol{J}_{c}^{\prime}\right)&,\ \text{when}\ \upgamma_{x}=1\\
\\
\displaystyle-\lambda\boldsymbol{C}_{z}^{-1}\bigg(\mathbf{\Omega}_{xx}+\boldsymbol{C}_{p}\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\bigg)&,\ \text{when}\ 0<\upgamma_{z}<\upgamma_{x}<1\\
\\
\displaystyle\lambda\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{r\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{xx}e^{r\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}dr\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\equiv\lambda\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}}&,\ \text{when}\ 0<\upgamma_{x}<\upgamma_{z}<1\end{cases}

where
\boldsymbol{B}_{p}(.)
is a
p-
dimensional standard Brownian motion,
\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{\lambda}e^{(\lambda-s)\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}d\boldsymbol{B}(s)
is an
Ornstein-Uhkenbeck
process and we denote with
\boldsymbol{J}^{\mu}_{c}(\lambda)=\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(\lambda)-\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(s)ds
and
\boldsymbol{B}_{p}^{\mu}(\lambda)=\boldsymbol{B}(\lambda)-\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{B}(s)ds
the demeaned processes of
\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(\lambda)
and
\boldsymbol{B}_{p}(\lambda)
respectively.

The weakly joint convergence result applies and the asymptotic terms given by expressions in
(i)
and
(ii)
are stochastically independent.

Proof.

We present the main conjectures for deriving the invariance principles presented by Lemma
4
(see,
Phillips and Magdalinos, 2009
and
Kostakis et al., 2015
for details)

\displaystyle n^{-(1+\upgamma_{z})}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}^{\prime}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\to}\boldsymbol{V}_{zz}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{r\boldsymbol{C}_{z}}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{xx}e^{r\boldsymbol{C}_{z}}dr

(7.94)

Moreover, we have the weakly convergence result from
Phillips and Magdalinos, 2009
:

\displaystyle n^{-\frac{1+\upgamma_{z}}{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\otimes\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\right)\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{V}_{zz}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}_{vv}\big)

(7.95)

Expression (
7.95
) shows weakly convergence into a mixed Gaussian limit distribution. In particular, this implies that the limit distribution of
n^{-(1+\upgamma_{z})/2}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\otimes\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\right)
is Gaussian with mean zero and covariance matrix equal to the probability limit of
n^{-(1+\upgamma_{z})/2}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{z}}_{t-1}\otimes\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\right)
, which is equal to
\boldsymbol{V}_{zz}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{vv}
, where
\boldsymbol{V}_{zz}
is defined in (
7.94
). Specifically, the above Mixed Gaussianity convergence argument, is a powerful property of the IVX filtration and ensures the robustness of the instrumental variable based procedure for abstract persistence. The dependence of the covariance matrix on the degree of persistence of the IVX instrument, induces exactly the Mixed Gaussianity. Similarly, the limit distribution below follows from Lemma 3.3 of PM.

\displaystyle n^{-(1+\upgamma_{z})/2}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\big(\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}\otimes\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\big)\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}\big(0,\boldsymbol{V}_{xx}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{vv}\big),\ \text{where}\ \boldsymbol{V}_{xx}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{r\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{xx}e^{r\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}dr

∎
